# Udaypur: The Complete Book Outline, Chapter 1 to the Afterword

*16 October 2026. One outline for the whole book. It supersedes, for planning, the Chapter 4–Afterword outline v2 (`Chapter_4_to_Afterword_Master_Drafting_Outline_v2.md`), which is kept unchanged as the record. The changes are listed in `OUTLINE_WHOLEBOOK_CHANGES.md`.*

## Purpose

This outline sets out, for every chapter of the Udaypur history, what each section establishes, from which evidence items, and under which book-level rules.
- **Chapters 1–4 are written.** Their entries are derived from the text as written: its sections, its paragraphs and the evidence each paragraph cites.
- **Chapters 5 to the Afterword are planned.**

Both are given in one format, so the book can be read as a single design. Only the status table shows which chapters are finished.

Every direct item in the two evidence maps has one of three statuses (Appendix):
- assigned to a paragraph;
- a cross-reference to its home elsewhere;
- excluded, with a reason.

The outline was built without paid model calls or vector-store queries; its checks are free scripts.

## How to read this document

- **Part I** holds everything book-wide:
  - the user's decisions, U1–U13, dated;
  - the evidence rules, R1–R11;
  - the homes of the recurring conflicts and of the shared evidence;
  - the boundaries between chapters;
  - the length decisions;
  - the retired material;
  - the decisions still needed;
  - the resolution log.

  Chapter entries cite these by number and never restate them.
- **Part II** has one entry per chapter:
  - function;
  - arc, with the opening and closing moves;
  - sections, each with its statement, its paragraphs (title, point, and evidence line) and its transition;
  - conclusion;
  - evidence checklist;
  - the chapter's own research tasks;
  - cross-chapter notes.

  A section heading gives its scope and target, and for a written chapter also its length as written.
- **Evidence lines** give item IDs by role:
  - **E** establishes, in its home unit only;
  - **C** corroborates or cross-refers;
  - **X** gives context;
  - **Note** feeds a footnote.

  Tiers are in brackets: T1 is the corpus of Paramāra inscriptions (CII), T5 the scholarly monographs, T7 the Gazetteer and the surveys, T8 testimony and tradition, and T9 popular accounts.
- **Parts III–VI:**
  - the chain of chapters and the threads;
  - the drafting protocol;
  - the single Research Register;
  - the continuity checklist.
- **The Appendix** gives each chapter's coverage by sub-topic and its exclusion list.

## The book's through-line

The book begins in an ordinary village and asks how it came to hold the remains, records and memories of a far more important place (Ch 1). It then follows the evidence outward and back:
- **The land** gave the town its stone and made it build its water, without deciding anything (Ch 2).
- **The country** was old, named and carved long before, but its past reaches the hill only as proposals, and by the tenth century its range was a crowded frontier (Ch 3).
- **The founder.** A Paramāra house took that frontier, and Bhoja's brother Udayāditya, recovering the kingdom after a three-sided war, put his name on a town, a temple and a tank. The record makes the name and the temple certain by 1080; it does not make the town his creation (Ch 4).
- **The building and the writing.** The book reads the temple for its own making (Ch 5) and the inscriptions for who made them speak (Ch 6).
- **The rest of the place:**
  - the town's other works, read as layers more than as a calendar (Ch 7);
  - the gifts that kept the god (Ch 8);
  - the stories the town tells of itself (Ch 9).
- **The later centuries.** Udaypur did not fall into ruin. It lost rank in steps under successive masters, the Caulukya kings of Gujarat among the first (Ch 10).
- **The modern record.** The place was described, collected and protected in pieces from 1871 to 2021 (Ch 11), and it lives on as a working village in an old town, seen mostly by others (Ch 12).

The Afterword turns, once and labelled, from history to argument: a place that survived by being used is best conserved as a lived-in town and hill.

Two questions run open through the whole book: whether Udayāditya founded Udaypur or renamed an older place, and how far a king's claims in stone can be taken at their word.

# Part I. Decisions, evidence rules and homes

## I.1 Decisions (settled)

| # | Decision | Date | Effect |

| U1 | Rājyapāla and Lūṇapasāka governed Udaypur as officers of the **Caulukya kings of Gujarat** (C10-PARTEMPLES-001; C10-CII-002; C08-GANGULY-015; C08-GANGULY-016). The Paramāra *mahākumāra* branch held the region "at least up to 1157" (C10-CII-002). Caulukya overlordship is attested at Udaypur from V.S. 1220 (1163), under Kumārapāla, to the early 1170s, under Ajayapāla. No date for the return of Paramāra rule at Udaypur is recorded (C04-GANGULY-135). | 15 Oct 2026 | §1.2, §1.3 and §3.4 written to it. ## I.2 Evidence rules

Chapter entries cite these by number and do not restate them.

- **R1. Three late records, kept separate.** Three objects are never identified with one another, because no source does so. A draft may compare them but never merges them.
  - **Record A, "the 1513 record":** Ganguly's fragmentary record "dated 1513 A.D." (*JAOS* VII p. 35). It states that Udayāditya built the temple in V.S. 1116; Ganguly calls it "very perfunctorily executed". Items: C01-GANGULY-020, C04-GANGULY-093.
  - **Record B, "the Udayapura pedigree":** CII dates it V.S. 1562 (1505/06) and rejects its genealogy (Śūravīra–Gōndala–Gyātā–Udayāditya) in view of the brother statement (U11). It states that Udayāditya was "ruling in Sam 1116, or Śaka 981". Items: C04-CII-131, C04-CII-132, C04-GANGULY-079, C04-GANGULY-081, C04-GANGULY-094, C01-CII-014.
  - **Record C, "the long V.S. 1116 inscription":** twenty-two lines on the temple, read as V.S. 1116 / Śaka 981 by S. K. Verma (reported by Tiwari) and by INTACH, and described as faulty in language and execution. Items: C06-JAGTA-EN-011, C06-INTACH-001, and C04-GAZ-006 (the Gazetteer's "two other inscriptions", which are not identified with C).
- **R2. Karṇa is always named by dynasty:** the **Kalacuri** king of Dāhala (c. 1041–72) or the **Caulukya** king of Gujarat (1064–94). Where an item does not say which Karṇa it means, the text keeps him unspecified and attributes the statement, and the item is listed in Part V, RR-K.
- **R3. The V.S. 1116 statements are not one record.** Record A's building date, Record B's "ruling in Sam 1116" and Record C's date stay distinct, under the labels A, B and C, until they are checked against the primary reports (Bühler, *EI* I; Cunningham, ASR IX–X). That check is RR-1.
- **R4. Administrative geography by date.** No administrative, territorial or institutional term is applied before the date of its earliest attesting record.
  - The Bhāillasvāmi-mahādvādaśaka and the Bhṛṅgārikā *pāṭhaka* (sixty-four villages) are attested in Lūṇapasāka's grant of the early 1170s (C01-GANGULY-010; C01-GAZ-010; C04-GAZ-007). They are never applied to Udayāditya's reign.
  - The "eastern division" governed from Udayapura is attested for 1163 (C08-GANGULY-016).
  - The Mahādvādaśaka-maṇḍala is a twelfth-century name (C04-BHOJDEV-HI-011).
  - The models are §1.2 P4, §3.4 P4 and §4.5 P4.
- **R5. Testimony.** Tiwari's testimony (JAGTA) is never a measurement, distance, count, date or medieval fact. Verma's readings, as Tiwari reports them, are attributed as such. Tiwari is named in full only in §1.1, with no gendered pronouns.
- **R6. One primary home; compatibility is not identity.**
  - An item establishes (E) a claim in one unit only, its home: the unit of its ID prefix unless I.4 names another.
  - Other units cite it as C and tell it in a clause.
  - No two inscriptions, buildings, tanks, streams, traditions, rulers, places, layers or hypotheses are merged unless an item says they are one.
- **R7. Claims stay claims.** A praśasti's statements are what the dynasty claimed. A tradition is evidence for memory. A survey's dates and identifications are proposals. Tier-7 sources are phrased as their own (U8).
- **R8. Proportion.** The Gazetteer supplies at most 40% of a section's cited items. The QC script warns above that.
- **R9. Excluded and staged sources.**
  - *Temple Economics* is excluded (its Udaipur is in Rajasthan).
  - SAMAR items (⚠, old OCR) are normative, are never quoted, and carry the attribution doubt.
  - The staged public-domain sources (Bühler, Beglar, Cunningham, the 1925–26 Gwalior report) are not evidence until hand-corrected and ingested.
- **R10. Settled ranges.** Udaypur lies 13–16 km from Ganj Basoda and 55–70 km from Vidisha. A single figure from one source is not substituted for the range. The 70 km end leans partly on testimony (RR-34).
- **R11. Present-day science stays present.** Modern climate, streamflow and land-use studies describe the present regime only. They are never read back into the eleventh century (§2.1–§2.2 are the models; the trends go in notes).

## I.5 Boundary rulings and the chain of chapters

- **Ch 1 | Ch 2.** Ch 1 ends on the stone and water that both founding answers stand on. Ch 2 opens on the river of the inscriptions and asks what the land gave and withheld. Ch 2 never makes the land a cause.
- **Ch 2 | Ch 3.** Ch 2 ends on what the ground does not say. Ch 3 opens on the oldest traces in the valleys and says how little reaches the hill.
- **Ch 3 | Ch 4.** Ch 3 ends: "Who held the hill itself in those years is not known." Ch 4 opens on Sīyaka II's eastern limit at Vidisha and keeps the gap open.
- **Ch 4 | Ch 5.** Ch 4 ends on the temple as the reign's one dated work at Udaypur. Ch 5 opens on the building facing east, entered from the west. Ch 4 gives no description of the building.
- **Ch 4 | Ch 10.** Ch 4 ends the dynastic story with the succession facts (§4.5 P6). Naravarman's reign and everything after c. 1093/94 belong to Ch 10.
- **Ch 5 | Ch 6.** Ch 5 ends on the flag-bearer and the inscribed name at the east doorway, not read. Ch 6 alone opens at the east door, on the six lines of V.S. 1137.
- **Ch 6 | Ch 7.** Ch 6 ends on what the stones cannot say, with the inscribed mosques dated but undescribed. Ch 7 opens on the largest uninscribed work, the hill wall.
- **Ch 7 | Ch 8.** Ch 7 ends on Vikrama's tank and its uses. Ch 8 opens on the 1229 gift of land.
- **Ch 8 | Ch 9.** Ch 8 ends on the time-keepers' family and its service. Ch 9 opens on a lane of Hindu and Muslim neighbours, described by community.
- **Ch 9 | Ch 10.** Ch 9 ends on the remembered deserted market of Daulatpur, as memory only. Ch 10 opens on the V.S. 1220 record and tests traditions against the record, without retelling them.
- **Ch 9 | Ch 12.** Ch 9 holds community and memory; Ch 12 holds livelihoods and voice.
- **Ch 10 | Ch 11.** Ch 10 ends on the Gazetteer's "once a place of considerable importance", the phrase §1.1 quoted at the start of the book. Ch 11 opens on Beglar, 1871–72: the history of description.
- **Ch 11 | Ch 12.** Ch 11 keeps every administrative act to 2021. Ch 12 keeps the people.
- **Ch 11 | Afterword.** AW.2 compresses Ch 11 into a jurisdiction map and argues implications.
- **Ch 12 | Afterword.** Ch 12 has no recommendations. The Afterword opens on the earliest dated plan, Tiwari's 2019 letter.

Part III, A holds the chain table, with the checks.

# Part II. The chapter entries

Thirteen entries follow, Chapter 1 to the Afterword. Rules and decisions are cited by number (Part I); open tasks by register number (Part V). Each section heading gives its scope and its target.

# Chapter 1. Udaypur: A Small Town with a Long Memory

**Target:** 4,000 words (as written: 3,301 in 5 sections) · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch01.jsonl`, with KB items (C01-KB-…) and borrowed Ch 4, 8 and 10 items in 1.2–1.3 · **Rules:** R1–R7, R10, U1, U6, U10, U11, U13.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** How did an ordinary village in Basoda tahsil come to hold the remains, records and memories of a far more important place, and what kind of royal act stands at its beginning?
- **Argument:** An ordinary village lives inside a Paramāra king's town. Its remains, records and memories all reach back to one eleventh-century act of naming, by Udayāditya, Bhoja's brother (U11). The evidence leaves open whether that act founded the place or took it over. The chapter sets out the polity in outline (Ch 4 tells it in full), the layers of rule to 1784–85, how the place is known, and the open question.
- **Establishes for later chapters:**
  - the village today (census 2011, the walled town, the time-keepers, Berni Mata, the fair);
  - the polity and the crisis, the kinship, the triad of names;
  - the governance terms;
  - U1 (Rājyapāla and Lūṇapasāka under the Caulukyas);
  - the later sequence to the Shahi Masjid and the brass cover;
  - the praśasti's slabs, the 1080 readings and the first readers;
  - the survival legends (told, not tested);
  - the founded-or-renamed question, with the Bhujariya Talab, the eighth-century inscription and Record A.

  Later chapters recall these in clauses only (Part I, I.4).

## 2. Arc

The present puzzle (1.1) → what the place was: a king's town on the eastern edge of the Paramāra kingdom (1.2) → how it changed across eight centuries (1.3) → how it is known (1.4) → the question the evidence leaves open, ending on the stone and water both answers stand on (1.5, into Ch 2).

The scale moves from the village to the kingdom and back to the ground.

**Opening:** “On the road from Ganj Basoda, hillocks begin to dot the horizon almost ten kilometres out, and two of them belong to Udaypur; nearer the village, one proves to be the big cylindrical hill known for its shape as Shiv pindi.”

**Closing:** “Whoever first lived here, under his rule or before it, had to make terms with that stone and that water.”

## 3. Sections

### 1.1 The village under the temple *(the town today · 850 w; as written 772)*
- **Statement:** A large, ordinary village lives inside the gateways, lanes and memories of a much greater medieval town. Its residents keep the founder's name and his temple in worship but hold little of the history. Tiwari is named in full here, once (R5).
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **The approach.** *Point:* the road from Ganj Basoda, the Shiv pindi hill, the temple in sight before the village; the 1979 compilers' "once a place of considerable importance".
     - E: C01-JAGTA-EN-013 (T8), C01-KB-GEOHER-004 (T7) · C: C01-GAZ-007 (T7), C01-MADHYABHARAT-009 (T5) · X: C01-GAZ-008 (T7)
  2. **A village, by its counts.** *Point:* panchayat, census 2011 (6,383), houses, schools and market as in 1979.
     - E: C01-GEOHER-002 (T7), C01-KB-GAZ-002 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-003 (T7) · C: C01-GEOHER-001 (T7), C01-GEOHER-003 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-009 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-010 (T7)
  3. **Inside the walled town.** *Point:* the gates, Teen Darwaza, lanes narrowed by houses, dry-masonry dwellings, the twelve-courtyard house.
     - E: C01-KB-GEOHER-001 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-002 (T7), C01-KB-JAGTA-HI-001 (T8) · C: C01-KB-INTACH-001 (T7), C01-KB-JAGTA-HI-002 (T8), C01-KB-JAGTA-HI-003 (T8)
  4. **Keepers of time and of memory.** *Point:* the Ghadiyali time-keepers; the remembered water tally (memory, R5); the tank's bed as fields (Tiwari); Berni Mata and her legend, and Vyas's *yakṣa* (note).
     - E: C01-GEOHER-013 (T7), C01-JAGTA-EN-024 (T8), C01-JAGTA-EN-033 (T8), C01-PANDE-006 (T5) · C: C01-GEOHER-012 (T7), C01-GEOHER-014 (T7), C01-JAGTA-EN-044 (T8), C01-JAGTA-HI-009 (T8) · X: C01-KB-GEOHER-015 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-016 (T7)
  5. **The temple still in worship.** *Point:* the east-facing temple in its walled court under the ASI; daily worship and the fair; Hindus and Muslims at the shrine in the 1870s; the name confused with Udaipur; the snake-and-fire founding legend.
     - E: C01-GANGULY-011 (T5), C01-JAGTA-EN-025 (T8), C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-001 (T5), C01-PANDE-010 (T5) · C: C01-GANGULY-012 (T5), C01-GEOHER-005 (T7), C01-JAGTA-EN-001 (T8), C01-JAGTA-EN-030 (T8), C01-JAGTA-EN-039 (T8), C01-PANDE-004 (T5)
- **Transition:** The name the village keeps is a king's; whose kingdom it was is the next question.

### 1.2 A king's town on Malwa's eastern edge *(the Paramāras and the founding · 850 w; as written 635)*
- **Statement:** The Paramāras are a kingdom, not an adjective: ruled from Dhārā, reaching Bhilsa on the east, nearly lost at Bhoja's death, recovered by his brother Udayāditya (U11) c. 1070 (working position). The town, temple and tank share one name (dates as readings, U6). In the 1160s–70s the town was the seat of an eastern division under the Caulukyas (U1, R4). The familiar forms of the dynasty names are given here once (U10).
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **A kingdom ruled from Dhārā.** *Point:* the lords of Malwa and their eastern reach to Bhilsa, where they met the Kalacuris and Candellas; Udaypur among the towns that grew under them.
     - E: C01-KB-GANGULY-009 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-010 (T5) · C: C01-BHOJA-TIMES-006 (T5)
  2. **Bhoja's fall and the recovery.** *Point:* the war, Jayasiṃha restored with Cālukya help, the Kalacuri Karṇa's return; Udayāditya, Bhoja's brother by two inscriptions of his son's time (U11; the dissent in a note); the accession c. 1070 with the other datings in a note; the Nagpur Boar image as the dynasty's telling, its Karṇa identified only by the editor (U13, RR-K); the realm recovered.
     - E: C01-KB-CII-007 (T1), C01-KB-GANGULY-004 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-008 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-011 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-012 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-013 (T5) · C: C01-KB-CII-003 (T1), C01-KB-CII-017 (T1), C04-CII-084 (T1), C04-CII-132 (T1), C04-GANGULY-077 (T5), C04-PANDE-005 (T5) · Note: C01-CII-013 (T1), C01-CII-014 (T1), C01-GANGULY-026 (T5), C01-KB-PARTEMPLES-001 (T5), C01-KRAM-2-002 (T5), C01-MADHYABHARAT-011 (T5), C04-BHOJA-TIMES-029 (T5), C04-CII-086 (T1), C04-CII-094 (T1), C04-CII-095 (T1), C04-CII-105 (T1), C04-GANGULY-076 (T5), C04-GANGULY-091 (T5), C04-GAZ-003 (T7)
  3. **One name for a town, a temple and a tank.** *Point:* Udayapura, Udayeśvara, Udayasamudra; V.S. 1116 and 1137 as readings; the lord's-seat or new-king question stated once.
     - E: C01-GANGULY-002 (T5), C01-HARDY-004 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-001 (T5), C01-MADHYABHARAT-001 (T5), C01-MADHYABHARAT-012 (T5) · C: C01-GANGULY-021 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-007 (T5), C01-PARTEMPLES-001 (T5)
  4. **How the east was governed.** *Point:* *maṇḍala*, *bhoga*, *pāṭhaka*, *viṣaya* and their officers; the Bhāillasvāmi-mahādvādaśaka in a record of the early 1170s; Rājyapāla's eastern division under Kumārapāla (U1); the Nēmaka house in a damaged record; Ganguly's junior line in a note.
     - E: C01-CII-012 (T1), C01-GANGULY-010 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-014 (T5), C01-KB-GANGULY-015 (T5) · C: C01-CII-013 (T1), C01-GANGULY-023 (T5), C01-KB-BHOJA-TIMES-003 (T5), C01-KB-JAGTA-EN-002 (T8), C08-GANGULY-016 (T5), C10-CII-002 (T1), C10-PARTEMPLES-001 (T5)
- **Transition:** The kingdom outlived its founder by two centuries, and every regime after it built inside his precinct.

### 1.3 Eight centuries in one precinct *(layers of rule, 1133–1784/85 · 850 w; as written 711)*
- **Statement:** Udaypur's layers placed in time: the Paramāra decline and the Caulukya hold through the town (U1); Devapāla resident in the early 1230s; 1305 and the mosque of 1336–38 in the temple court; Mandu and Mughal building to 1632 and the governor's mansion; the brass cover of V.S. 1841; custody today.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **The kingdom breaks.** *Point:* Yaśovarman and Jayasiṃha of Gujarat; the Candella Madanavarman at Bhāillasvāmi, 1134; the *mahākumāra*s; Udaypur under the Caulukyas and Lūṇapasāka's grant (U1); Devapāla's two records and his residence at Udayapura; Jayavarman to 1269.
     - E: C01-GANGULY-005 (T5), C01-GANGULY-010 (T5), C01-KB-CII-014 (T1), C01-KB-GANGULY-017 (T5), C01-KB-PARTEMPLES-004 (T5) · C: C01-CII-010 (T1), C01-GANGULY-016 (T5), C01-KB-CII-009 (T1), C01-KB-CII-010 (T1), C01-KB-CII-012 (T1), C01-KB-CII-013 (T1), C01-KB-GANGULY-018 (T5), C08-GANGULY-015 (T5), C10-CII-002 (T1), C10-PARTEMPLES-001 (T5) · X: C01-KB-CII-008 (T1)
  2. **1305, and the mosque in the court.** *Point:* Iltutmish and ʿAin-ul-Mulk; the court's plan; the mosque of A.H. 737–739 on the western hall; the tradition of 1325; Tughluq against the Aurangzeb legend (note).
     - E: C01-KB-GANGULY-019 (T5), C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-009 (T5), C01-PANDE-005 (T5) · C: C01-KB-PANDE-003 (T5), C01-MADHYABHARAT-005 (T5), C01-PANDE-003 (T5), C01-PANDE-013 (T5), C07-GANGULY-003 (T5), C07-PANDE-009 (T5), C10-PARTEMPLES-003 (T5)
  3. **Mandu and the Mughals.** *Point:* Hoshang Shah's move to Mandu; the walls and fortified hilltop; Sher Khan's agent's mosque, 1488; the Shahi Masjid, 1632; the governor's mansion (its founder disputed; note under U7).
     - E: C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-002 (T5), C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-006 (T5) · C: C01-GEOHER-007 (T7), C01-JAGTA-EN-017 (T8), C01-JAGTA-EN-026 (T8), C01-KB-GEOHER-011 (T7), C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-010 (T5), C07-MADHYABHARAT-011 (T5), C07-MADHYABHARAT-012 (T5)
  4. **Later patrons, and custody now.** *Point:* the brass cover, V.S. 1841 (RR-2); "Garde Sahib" (Tiwari; RR-33); the state's stakeholders; Adhikari's and Hardy's assessments (note).
     - E: C01-INTACH-006 (T7), C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-007 (T5), C01-PANDE-011 (T5) · C: C01-GEOHER-005 (T7), C01-HARDY-002 (T5), C01-KB-JAGTA-EN-004 (T8), C01-MADHYABHARAT-013 (T5), C01-PARTEMPLES-004 (T5), C07-INTACH-005 (T7), C13-GEOHER-004 (T7)
- **Transition:** What survives of the founder's own voice is a broken slab of praise; how the place is known comes next.

### 1.4 How Udaypur is known *(the record · 750 w; as written 636)*
- **Statement:** The kinds of evidence behind the story and what each can bear: the praśasti's two slabs and its claims; the 1080 record, read three ways and the earliest firm date (U6); the pilgrims' inscriptions and the first modern readers; the survival legends as memory.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **Two broken slabs, and the dates.** *Point:* the praśasti, its agnikula opening (legend, in the editor's judgement), its uncorroborated claims; the slabs' finding (Garde, 1925–26) and the second slab's rout of Chedi; V.S. 1116 and 1137 as readings and 1080 as the earliest known date (U6); the three readings of 1080; the "1059—1080" caption claims too much.
     - E: C01-CII-007 (T1), C01-CII-009 (T1), C01-GANGULY-008 (T5), C01-GANGULY-009 (T5), C01-GANGULY-025 (T5), C01-GEOHER-006 (T7), C01-KB-CII-004 (T1), C01-KB-CII-015 (T1), C01-MADHYABHARAT-012 (T5), C01-PARTEMPLES-005 (T5) · C: C01-BHOJA-TIMES-004 (T5), C01-BHOJA-TIMES-009 (T5), C01-CII-008 (T1), C01-GANGULY-006 (T5), C01-KB-CII-001 (T1), C01-KB-CII-016 (T1), C01-KB-GANGULY-005 (T5), C01-KRAM-1-001 (T5) · Note: C01-HARDY-004 (T5), C01-KB-CII-002 (T1)
  2. **Pilgrims and readers.** *Point:* inscriptions across eight centuries in three languages; Turasadasa, V.S. 1696; Beglar, Cunningham, Bühler; the reset slab.
     - E: C01-KB-GEOHER-006 (T7), C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-003 (T5), C01-KB-PANDE-001 (T5), C01-KB-PANDE-002 (T5) · C: C01-GEOHER-010 (T7), C01-KB-SERPENT-001 (T5), C01-PANDE-002 (T5), C01-PANDE-012 (T5), C01-SERPENT-002 (T5)
  3. **Stories of survival.** *Point:* Tughluq's gunpowder, Aurangzeb, a night's building, treasure, the flour-grinder woman: memory of survival, not of founding (tested in §10.3).
     - E: C01-GANGULY-013 (T5), C01-JAGTA-EN-043 (T8), C01-KB-GANGULY-002 (T5), C01-PANDE-003 (T5) · C: C01-JAGTA-EN-029 (T8), C01-JAGTA-EN-034 (T8), C01-MADHYABHARAT-006 (T5)
- **Transition:** These records begin with Udayāditya's name, but cannot say whether he founded the place or renamed it.

### 1.5 Founded or taken over? *(the open question · 700 w; as written 547)*
- **Statement:** The founding question is genuine: proposals for an older place (the Bhujariya Talab, the eighth-century inscription, reused columns, the layered fabric) against the argument from absence and the traditions of a conquered place. Both answers describe different acts of rule; the later records cannot choose. The chapter ends on stone and water.
transition (out): Into Ch 2: the stone and the water the founder had to make terms with.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **An older place proposed.** *Point:* the Bhujariya Talab, the two-line inscription, the columns, the uncemented wall, the layers from the sixth century (INTACH proposals; note).
     - E: C01-KB-GEOHER-007 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-008 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-012 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-013 (T7), C01-KB-GEOHER-014 (T7) · C: C01-GEOHER-021 (T7), C01-INTACH-006 (T7) · X: C01-GAZ-017 (T7)
  2. **A new town argued.** *Point:* the argument from absence (Tiwari) and his frontier post; Bhringrajpur and the Gond tradition, told from the founder's side.
     - E: C01-GEOHER-008 (T7), C01-GEOHER-011 (T7), C01-JAGTA-EN-042 (T8), C01-JAGTA-EN-050 (T8) · C: C01-GEOHER-009 (T7), C01-GEOHER-022 (T7)
  3. **Two acts of rule.** *Point:* founding or re-inscription; Udhapuram, the sixteenth-century praise and Record A (R1); none speaks of the place before the name.
     - E: C01-GANGULY-007 (T5), C01-GANGULY-018 (T5), C01-GANGULY-020 (T5) · C: C01-KB-GANGULY-004 (T5)
  4. **Stone and water.** *Point:* the quarry beside the older tank; tanks of more than one age.
     - E: C01-KB-GEOHER-008 (T7) · C: C01-GEOHER-021 (T7)

# Chapter 2. The Betwa Valley: Geography, Geology and Natural Environment

**Target:** 4,000 words (as written: 2,608 in 5 sections; below the 3,200 floor, evidence-limited, open issue RR-35) · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch02.jsonl` (wildlife items moved to Ch 12) · **Rules:** R5–R8, R11.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** What could this country give, and what did it withhold, to the people who settled and built at Udaypur, and what did kings and villagers make of it?
- **Argument:** The land the founder found had no river at its door and no dependable groundwater. It had one wet season, black soil and a hill of building stone. Its water had to be made, and its town was built of its own rock. The land is the setting, never the cause. Modern climate and flow data describe the present regime only.
- **Establishes for later chapters:**
  - the Betwa (Vētravatī) and the Kewtan;
  - the rains and the two harvests;
  - the Garhi–Teonda range and the sandstone "quarried in the hill close by";
  - the forest and its grazing;
  - Bhojpur, as comparison only;
  - Udaypur's tanks, *kund*s and stepwells at landscape level (detail in Ch 7);
  - the quarries and Govind Saxena's report.

## 2. Arc

Where the water runs and where it does not (2.1) → the rains and the farming year (2.2) → the seam of stone (2.3) → water made by kings and villagers (2.4) → a town of its own stone (2.5, into Ch 3).

**Opening:** “In the Paramāra inscriptions the Betwa appears as the Vētravatī, the river that flows by Vidisha and the city of Bhilsa.”

**Closing:** “It does not decide who settles on it, or when, or why; and whether anyone had used it before Udaypur had its name, the ground itself does not say.”

## 3. Sections

### 2.1 Where the water runs, and where it does not *(rivers and groundwater · 850 w; as written 595)*
- **Statement:** Udaypur belongs to the Betwa's country but not to its banks. The river is intermittent upstream and the sandstone's wells fail in summer, so the place depends on water gathered in natural hollows.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **The river of the inscriptions.** *Point:* the Vētravatī by Vidisha and Bhilsa; Bhilsa as Bhoja's eastern limit (note); Lakṣmīvarman against the Candellas across the Betwa (this sentence carries a duplicated fragment, RR-36); the province of Bhilsa in the twelfth century.
     - E: C02-CII-001 (T1), C02-CII-002 (T1), C02-KB-GANGULY-002 (T5), C02-KB-MADHYABHARAT-001 (T5) · C: C01-KB-GANGULY-016 (T5), C02-KB-PARTEMPLES-001 (T5)
  2. **The Betwa's course, and the Kewtan.** *Point:* source, course and tributaries; the district sloping north; the Kewtan 1 km from the village; no river through Udaypur.
     - E: C02-GEOHER-001 (T7), C02-GEOHER-002 (T7) · C: C02-GAZ-001 (T7), C02-GAZ-002 (T7), C02-GAZ-003 (T7)
  3. **A river of the rains.** *Point:* the present regime: intermittent upstream, monsoon runoff.
     - E: C02-BETWA-FLOW-002 (T5), C02-BETWA-FLOW-003 (T5), C02-KB-BETWA-FLOW-001 (T5) · C: C02-BETWA-CLIM-001 (T5), C02-BETWA-CLIM-004 (T5)
  4. **Groundwater that fails.** *Point:* the sandstone and its wells in 1979; piped water only at Vidisha.
     - E: C02-GAZ-053 (T7), C02-GEOHER-003 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-004 (T7) · C: C02-KB-GAZ-010 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-011 (T7)
  5. **Water in the hollows.** *Point:* lakes and ponds in depressions; settlement shaped by water.
     - E: C02-GEOHER-001 (T7), C02-GEOHER-028 (T7)
- **Transition:** What the river would not give, the monsoon gave once a year.

### 2.2 The rains and the farming year *(climate, soil and harvest · 850 w; as written 595)*
- **Statement:** The year's water arrives in four months and bad years come in runs. The black soil, the wells and the tanks carry the winter crop. The modern climate trend appears only in a note.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **Four months of rain.** *Point:* 92 per cent in the monsoon (1979 record); evaporation exceeds rainfall in the basin.
     - E: C02-BETWA-CLIM-004 (T5), C02-GAZ-015 (T7), C02-GAZ-016 (T7) · C: C02-GAZ-014 (T7), C02-GAZ-021 (T7), C02-GAZ-022 (T7)
  2. **Bad years in runs.** *Point:* 1901–50; the famine of 1899–1900; the Basoda deluge of 1951.
     - E: C02-GAZ-017 (T7), C02-GAZ-018 (T7), C02-GAZ-019 (T7) · C: C02-GAZ-023 (T7)
  3. **The soil and the two harvests.** *Point:* black *mar* soil "very good for wheat"; *kharif* and *rabi*; the district by 1971–72; Udaypur's crops at the survey; wells and tanks for the winter crop.
     - E: C02-GAZ-040 (T7), C02-GEOHER-027 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-005 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-006 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-007 (T7) · C: C02-GAZ-013 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-013 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-014 (T7)
  4. **Silt, and what is not known.** *Point:* the river *doab*s; whether silt reaches Udaypur is not recorded; the climate trend in a note.
     - E: C02-GAZ-045 (T7), C02-GAZ-046 (T7), C02-GAZ-047 (T7) · C: C02-GAZ-052 (T7) · Note: C02-BETWA-CLIM-007 (T5), C02-BETWA-CLIM-011 (T5), C02-BETWA-FLOW-004 (T5), C02-BETWA-FLOW-006 (T5), C02-KB-BETWA-FLOW-002 (T5)
- **Transition:** Where the rain gathers or runs off is decided by the rock beneath it.

### 2.3 A seam of stone *(geology, landform and forest · 900 w; as written 633)*
- **Statement:** Udaypur sits where erosion stripped the trap from bedded sandstone. The temple's sandstone was quarried in the hill close by. The hills are remnants of a wasting tableland, dry on top and hollowed by caves. The thin forest has long been grazed and cut.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **Foothills of the Garhi–Teonda range.** *Point:* heights; the plateau; the range between the Betwa and the Bina.
     - E: C02-GAZ-007 (T7), C02-GEOHER-005 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-008 (T7), C02-KB-GAZ-009 (T7) · C: C02-GEOHER-006 (T7)
  2. **Trap stripped from sandstone.** *Point:* the Deccan trap and the Vindhyan beds; the temple's stone "quarried in the hill close by"; sandstone across the district; Patil's geography of monuments (note).
     - E: C02-GAZ-008 (T7), C02-GAZ-010 (T7), C02-PANDE-001 (T5) · C: C02-GAZ-042 (T7), C02-GAZ-050 (T7), C02-GEOHER-013 (T7), C02-MADHYABHARAT-003 (T5)
  3. **A wasting tableland.** *Point:* mesa, butte and pinnacle as a proposed pathway; Shiva Pindi; overhangs and caves; the survey's inconsistent heights (note; RR-37).
     - E: C02-GEOHER-008 (T7), C02-GEOHER-009 (T7), C02-GEOHER-010 (T7), C02-GEOHER-016 (T7) · C: C02-GEOHER-007 (T7), C02-GEOHER-011 (T7), C02-GEOHER-012 (T7), C02-GEOHER-014 (T7), C02-GEOHER-015 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-010 (T7) · X: C02-GEOHER-020 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-008 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-009 (T7) · Note: C02-GEOHER-005 (T7)
  4. **The forest on the slopes.** *Point:* the district's forest in 1979; the reserved forest above Udaypur, grazed and cut.
     - E: C02-GAZ-012 (T7), C02-GAZ-027 (T7), C02-GEOHER-021 (T7) · C: C02-GAZ-030 (T7), C02-GAZ-037 (T7), C02-GEOHER-019 (T7) · X: C02-KB-GEOHER-012 (T7)
- **Transition:** Of all the land offered, the town took its stone; its water it had to make.

### 2.4 Water made by kings and villagers *(storage · 900 w; as written 632)*
- **Statement:** Storing water against the dry months was Paramāra practice: Bhoja's dam on the Betwa as comparison; the treatise's norms (attribution doubted, R9). At Udaypur town, temple and tank shared one name, and the water was sorted by use. Landscape level only; the detail is in Ch 7.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **Bhoja's lake, by comparison.** *Point:* the Bhojpur dams and the lake's breach by a Mandu sultan (variants in notes).
     - E: C02-BHOJA-TIMES-001 (T5), C02-GANGULY-001 (T5), C02-RAJABHOJ-EN-003 (T9) · C: C02-BHOJA-TIMES-002 (T5), C02-KB-BHOJA-TIMES-001 (T5) · Note: C02-BHOJA-TIMES-005 (T5), C02-KB-JAGTA-EN-001 (T8), C02-RAJABHOJ-EN-001 (T9)
  2. **Water in the treatise.** *Point:* the three kinds of country; the land praised for a town; nothing ties the verses to Udaypur's site (norm, R9).
     - E: C02-BHOJDEV-HI-002 (T9), C02-BHOJDEV-HI-004 (T9), C02-BHOJDEV-HI-005 (T9) · Note: C02-KB-RAJABHOJ-EN-002 (T9), C02-KB-SAMAR-1-001 (T4)
  3. **Udaypur's water, sorted by use.** *Point:* the triad's tank; tanks for fields, wells for drinking; *kund*s on the hill; stepwells with proposed dates; the "38 tanks"; the Bhujariya Talab as possibly older.
     - E: C02-GEOHER-029 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-001 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-005 (T7) · C: C02-KB-GAZ-012 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-002 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-003 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-007 (T7), C02-KB-GEOHER-018 (T7), C02-KB-INTACH-001 (T7), C02-KB-MADHYABHARAT-003 (T5)
- **Transition:** The water was made; the town that held it was cut from its own hill.

### 2.5 A town of its own stone *(stone, then and now · 500 w; as written 153)*
- **Statement:** The temple, the dry-masonry houses, the shops and today's quarries all come from the hill, and the quarrying now presses on the land. The chapter closes on the setting a founder found.
transition (out): Into Ch 3: whether anyone used the country before Udaypur had its name.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **A town of its own stone.** *Point:* dry masonry; Tiwari's stone shops; the quarries' export trade; illegal quarrying (Saxena's report).
     - E: C02-JAGTA-EN-001 (T8), C02-JAGTA-EN-002 (T8), C02-KB-GEOHER-006 (T7) · C: C02-JAGTA-EN-003 (T8), C02-JAGTA-HI-004 (T8), C02-PANDE-001 (T5)
  2. **What the ground does not say.** *Point:* stone at hand and rain to be held; the ground decides nothing about who settled, or when.
- **Transition:** 

## 4. Conclusion

- **Demonstrated:** a country of made water and local stone; the land as setting.
- **Unresolved:**
  - whether silt reached Udaypur;
  - the survey's heights (RR-37);
  - the chapter's length (RR-35).
- **Carried forward:** the country's earlier users (Ch 3); tanks in detail (Ch 7); quarries and the hill today (Ch 12).

# Chapter 3. Before Udaypur: Prehistory, Early Vidisha and the Historical Landscape of Central India

**Target:** 3,550 words (as written: 2,557 in 5 sections; below the 3,200 floor, evidence-limited, open issue RR-35) · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch03.jsonl` (the Gazetteer at most 40% of any section, R8) · **Rules:** R5–R8.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** What was already in this country when a king put his name on Udaypur's hill, and how much of that long past can be found at the hill itself?
- **Argument:** Udaypur's hill stood in an old, named country whose centre, roads and carved shrines lay elsewhere. They reached the hill only through proposals. By the tenth century its own range had become a crowded frontier. Udaypur is the subject; Vidisha and Central India enter only where they change what Udaypur means.
- **Establishes for later chapters:**
  - Daśārṇa and Ākara;
  - Vidisha/Besnagar, the roads, Sanchi;
  - Heliodoros and early Vaiṣṇava and Śaiva images;
  - Udayagiri and the Gupta shrines;
  - Badoh, Pathari and the Seven Mothers panel;
  - the sixth-century cave on Udaypur's hill (proposal);
  - Bhilsa and Bhāillasvāmin;
  - the Rāṣṭrakūṭas at Pathari, 861;
  - Gyaraspur;
  - the Candellas at Bhāsvat, 954.

## 2. Arc

Old ground and older names (3.1) → the old centre and its roads (3.2) → carved rock and early shrines (3.3) → from Vidisha to Bhilsa (3.4) → a frontier on Udaypur's range (3.5, into Ch 4).

**Opening:** “The oldest human traces in the country around Udaypur lie in its river valleys.”

**Closing:** “Who held the hill itself in those years is not known.”

## 3. Sections

### 3.1 Old ground, older names *(prehistory and the first names · 550 w; as written 354)*
- **Statement:** The limits of the oldest evidence (no find placed on the hill) and what the country was called before Udaypur existed.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **Traces in the valleys.** *Point:* palaeolithic tools; an unnamed chalcolithic mound; the ASI's sequence near Vidisha; nothing on the hill; the caves recorded, their early use unknown.
     - E: C03-GAZ-001 (T7), C03-GAZ-002 (T7), C03-GAZ-003 (T7) · C: C03-KB-GEOHER-001 (T7)
  2. **Daśārṇa.** *Point:* the literary name and the Dhasan; Kauṭilya; Vidisha its capital.
     - E: C03-MADHYABHARAT-018 (T5) · C: C03-GAZ-041 (T7)
  3. **Ākara, and the earliest rulers named.** *Point:* the Nasik praśasti's Ākara and Avanti; the Haihayas and the Pradyotas (Patil's certainty, note).
     - E: C03-CII-002 (T1), C03-CII-004 (T1), C03-MADHYABHARAT-019 (T5) · C: C03-KB-MADHYABHARAT-001 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-001 (T5)
  4. **Vidisha at the centre.** *Point:* one sentence; Tiwari's temple-city in a note.
     - E: C03-MADHYABHARAT-018 (T5) · X: C03-JAGTA-EN-005 (T8)
- **Transition:** That centre was a city in the fork of the rivers.

### 3.2 The old centre and its roads *(Vidisha · 600 w; as written 444)*
- **Statement:** Vidisha as the country's centre for more than a thousand years, kept to what it means for Udaypur: roads and stone-working traditions.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **An old city in the fork.** *Point:* Besnagar, its excavation, the palace wall.
     - E: C03-GAZ-037 (T7), C03-GAZ-039 (T7), C03-MADHYABHARAT-015 (T5) · C: C03-MADHYABHARAT-017 (T5)
  2. **Śuṅga Vidisha.** *Point:* Agnimitra; Bhagabhadra; prosperity to the Guptas.
     - E: C03-MADHYABHARAT-003 (T5) · C: C03-MADHYABHARAT-010 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-017 (T5)
  3. **Two roads and a crossing.** *Point:* the trade routes; later itineraries; none mentions Udaypur's hill.
     - E: C03-GAZ-061 (T7), C03-MADHYABHARAT-029 (T5) · C: C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-008 (T9) · X: C03-GAZ-063 (T7), C03-KB-MADHYABHARAT-002 (T5)
  4. **Wealth in stone.** *Point:* Sanchi and the merchants; a religious centre.
     - E: C03-GAZ-009 (T7), C03-MADHYABHARAT-015 (T5) · C: C03-HARDY-001 (T5)
- **Transition:** The city's shrines began a tradition of worship in stone.

### 3.3 Carved rock and early shrines *(the sacred landscape · 900 w; as written 633)*
- **Statement:** The country around Udaypur as one of the oldest landscapes of worship in stone; carried to Udaypur's range, and to the hill only as proposals.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **The earliest temples of Viṣṇu.** *Point:* the elliptical temple; Heliodoros's Garuḍa pillar; Gautamīputra's flagstaff.
     - E: C03-GAZ-016 (T7), C03-GAZ-028 (T7), C03-KRAM-1-002 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-013 (T5) · C: C03-GAZ-027 (T7), C03-KRAM-1-001 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-007 (T5), C03-TEMPLES-IND-001 (T5) · Note: C03-GAZ-029 (T7)
  2. **Śaiva images before the Paramāras.** *Point:* the colossi; Besnagar's Durgā, Mothers and *liṅga*s (dating variants in notes).
     - E: C03-MADHYABHARAT-012 (T5) · C: C03-KB-MADHYABHARAT-007 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-004 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-006 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-008 (T5) · X: C03-MADHYABHARAT-005 (T5)
  3. **The Guptas in the hills.** *Point:* the Sanakānikas; Udayagiri and the Boar; flat-roofed shrines at Ramgarh, Chhapara, Badoh.
     - E: C03-HARDY-002 (T5), C03-KRAM-1-005 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-022 (T5), C03-TEMPLES-IND-004 (T5) · C: C03-KB-MADHYABHARAT-010 (T5), C03-KB-MADHYABHARAT-011 (T5), C03-KRAM-1-004 (T5), C03-MADHYABHARAT-025 (T5)
  4. **Onto Udaypur's range.** *Point:* the Seven Mothers panel between Badoh and Pathari.
     - E: C03-KB-MADHYABHARAT-006 (T5), C03-KB-MADHYABHARAT-009 (T5) · C: C02-KB-GAZ-009 (T7)
  5. **On the hill, a proposal.** *Point:* the cave assigned to the sixth century; the figures "believed" Jain; Tiwari's surmise.
     - E: C03-KB-INTACH-001 (T7), C03-KB-INTACH-002 (T7), C03-KB-INTACH-003 (T7) · C: C03-JAGTA-EN-007 (T8)
- **Transition:** Meanwhile the old city itself was emptying.

### 3.4 From Vidisha to Bhilsa *(a new town and its name · 650 w; as written 480)*
- **Statement:** The change from Vidisha to Bhilsa: still a royal seat in 608; the readings of the old city's end; Bhilsa's name from the Sun image Bhāillasvāmin; the name carried into Udaypur's later province (R4).
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **608 and after.** *Point:* the Vadner plate; the centre shifts across the Betwa.
     - E: C03-GAZ-053 (T7), C03-MADHYABHARAT-017 (T5) · Note: C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-003 (T9)
  2. **Readings of the end.** *Point:* deluge or political event; eighth–ninth century; branch-town (notes).
     - E: C03-KB-GAZ-001 (T7) · C: C03-GAZ-024 (T7) · Note: C03-GAZ-019 (T7), C03-KB-RAJABHOJ-EN-002 (T9)
  3. **A god's name for a town.** *Point:* Bhāillasvāmin; Al-Bīrūnī; the 878 records; Krishna III; Bhoja's *Śṛṅgāramañjarīkathā*.
     - E: C03-CII-001 (T1), C03-CII-004 (T1), C03-KB-CII-001 (T1) · C: C03-GAZ-054 (T7), C03-MADHYABHARAT-014 (T5) · X: C03-KB-RAJABHOJ-EN-001 (T9), C03-KB-RAJABHOJ-EN-003 (T9)
  4. **The name travels.** *Point:* the Mahādvādaśaka-maṇḍala; Lūṇapasāka's grant of the early 1170s (U1, R4).
     - C: C01-GANGULY-010 (T5), C03-KB-CII-002 (T1)
- **Transition:** By then the powers that mattered on Udaypur's range were nearer than Vidisha had ever been.

### 3.5 A frontier on Udaypur’s range *(eighth to tenth centuries · 850 w; as written 646)*
- **Statement:** Udaypur's own range as a frontier crowded with temples in the two centuries before the Paramāras. The hill's own record is one proposed inscription.

# Chapter 4. The Paramāras and the Foundation of Udaypur: Kingship, Patronage and Urban Formation

**Target:** 5,000 words (U9; as written: 4,054 in 5 sections, evidence-limited) · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch04.jsonl` · **Rules:** R1–R9, U1, U6, U8, U10, U11, U12. An approved revision to six sections (U12) is set out in RR-40.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** How did a frontier ridge on Malwa's eastern edge come to carry a king's name? What can the record say about whether Udayāditya made Udaypur before he was king, as king, or by putting his name on a place others held?
- **Argument:** The Paramāras reached this frontier as Rāṣṭrakūṭa feudatories who made themselves kings, and held it as their eastern limit under Bhoja. The town carries the name of Bhoja's brother (U11), who recovered the kingdom after a three-sided war, and a temple there bore his name by 1080. A beginning in 1059 rests on late or faulty records (R1, R3, U6). The town's physical making is not dated by this chapter's evidence.
- **Builds on:** §1.2 (the polity, the crisis, the kinship note, the triad, the province, U1); §1.4 (the praśasti, the 1080 readings); §1.5 (founded or taken over; Record A); §3.5 (the Candellas at Bhāsvat). Recalled in clauses only.
- **Establishes for Ch 5:** the Udayeśvara as the one securely dated royal work at Udaypur (the V.S. 1137 record), named after its constructor; its start date as a reading.

## 2. Arc

The frontier and the house that took it (4.1) → Bhoja's kingdom and Bhoja's name (4.2) → the broken succession, the three-sided war and who Udayāditya was (4.3) → the dates tested and the dated reign (4.4) → the town that bears his name, and the king's end (4.5, into Ch 5).

Chronology runs through 4.1–4.3, is suspended in 4.4, and returns at the end of 4.5.

**Opening:** “The next claim made about this country comes from the west.”

**Closing:** “Of all the works later writers give him at Udaypur, from the town's foundation to its walls and lakes, one is tied to his reign by a record of the time: the temple that bears his name, whose flag-hoisting a stone on the temple records in V.S. 1137.”

## 3. Sections

### 4.1 From feudatories to kings of Malwa *(c. 900–1010 · 700 w; as written 659)*
- **Statement:** The house first appears as Rāṣṭrakūṭa feudatories in Gujarat; a sovereign title brought war; the first charter from Malwa dates from 975; Udaypur's praśasti claims more for these kings than their grants and their enemies' records allow (R7).
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **The claim on the east.** *Point:* the Gazetteer credits Sīyaka II with ousting the Candellas and a kingdom bounded by Vidisha (U8); the limit is a kingdom's edge, not the hill's holder.
     - E: C04-CII-012 (T1), C04-GAZ-001 (T7), C04-PARTEMPLES-002 (T5) · C: C04-GANGULY-035 (T5)
  2. **Feudatories with a king's title.** *Point:* the 949 grants; the line back to c. 900; Ganguly's Rāṣṭrakūṭa vassalage (note).
     - E: C04-CII-002 (T1), C04-CII-003 (T1), C04-CII-006 (T1) · C: C04-CII-007 (T1) · Note: C04-GANGULY-015 (T5), C04-GANGULY-027 (T5)
  3. **Independence by war.** *Point:* the Rāṣṭrakūṭa expedition; Khalighaṭṭa; Mānyakheṭa plundered, 972–73; the first Malwa charter, 975.
     - E: C04-CII-004 (T1), C04-CII-012 (T1), C04-CII-042 (T1), C04-GANGULY-032 (T5) · C: C04-CII-009 (T1), C04-GANGULY-033 (T5)
  4. **What Udaypur's stone claims.** *Point:* Muñja's death as Tailapa II's captive against the praśasti's Karṇāṭas at his feet and Tripurī, judged inadmissible; Sindhurāja's half verse.
     - E: C04-CII-024 (T1), C04-CII-026 (T1), C04-CII-129 (T1) · C: C04-GANGULY-045 (T5) · Note: C04-GANGULY-039 (T5)
  5. **A genealogy kept at Udaypur.** *Point:* the only complete early line; conventional portraits; Upendra as the grants' Kṛṣṇarāja; the fire-pit birth set aside (note).
     - E: C04-CII-021 (T1), C04-CII-023 (T1), C04-GANGULY-023 (T5), C04-GANGULY-030 (T5) · C: C04-GANGULY-019 (T5)
- **Transition:** Within a generation the eastern limit belonged to a king whose name would outlast his records.

- **Statement:** Bhoja's dated reign and the Bhopal country in his kingdom by 1018; the model of royal building and learning his name carried; his fame at Udaypur as praise cut by his successors; the praśasti's turn from his death to Udayāditya.

## 4. Conclusion

- **Demonstrated:**
  - the house's rise and the frontier;
  - the omission of Jayasiṃha;
  - the three-sided war;
  - the kinship (U11);
  - 1080 as the firm date;
  - the Nēmaka seat;
  - the later province.
- **Unresolved:**
  - whether anything was built before the reign;
  - the enemies' identities (R2);
  - the builder of the Nēmaka temple;
  - founded or renamed;
  - when the walled town took shape.
- **Carried forward:** a king's name and a king's temple are certain, a king's town is not; the building is next read for what it says of its own making.

# Chapter 5. The Udayeśvara Temple: Architecture, Sculpture and Religious Iconography

**Target:** 4,000 words · **Sections:** 7 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch05.jsonl` (284 direct, 698 contextual) · **Rules:** R5–R9.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** What is the Udayeśvara by its own fabric, and what has its image programme been made to mean?
- **Argument:** The temple is the fullest statement of the Bhūmija system: a rotated-square star of seven tiers whose wall projections rise into the tower. Its walls carry a programme set by rule (dancers, guardians, Mothers) that Pande reads as Śaiva Siddhānta. The building is described from its fabric. Its meanings are attributed and bounded (R7). Its losses, the court's shrines and the western hall, are part of the fabric's evidence.
- **What changes:** from "the temple" as a name and a date (Ch 4) to a building read part by part, and then to the readings laid on it.
- **Builds on:** §1.1 P5 (the east-facing temple in its walled court, still in worship, under the ASI), §1.3 (the court described as 4 + 4, the mosque, Adhikari's verdict), §1.4 (the dates), §2.3 (the stone). Recalled in clauses only.
- **Establishes for Ch 6:** the east doorway with the flag-bearer and the god's name, where Ch 6 opens on the written record.

## 2. Arc

The court and the building as now met (5.1) → the geometry that makes the tower (5.2) → its place among the Bhūmija temples (5.3) → the surfaces as craft (5.4) → the gods on the walls by position (5.5) → the remaining images and the broken ones (5.6) → what the programme has been made to mean, ending at the east door (5.7).

Description comes before interpretation. Chronology is suspended: the building is one moment, its losses are later.

**Opening:** the temple faces east, but is entered today from the west (C05-PANDE-002, -011).

**Closing:** the flag-bearer and the inscribed name at the east doorway, not read (Ch 6 reads them).

## 3. Sections

### 5.1 The court and the building it holds *(plan, enclosure, losses · 750 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things:
  - the temple's eastern axis;
  - the hall and three porches;
  - the separate *vedī* before it;
  - the court of attendant shrines, of which some survive in ruin and some are gone (conflict home: the shrine count).
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **The design's own axis.** *Point:* sanctum, vestibule, hall, three porches, *vedī*, on an east–west line now met from the west.
     - E: C05-PANDE-001 (T5), C05-PANDE-002 (T5), C05-PANDE-003 (T5) · C: C05-PANDE-011 (T5), C05-MADHYABHARAT-005 (T5), C05-GANGULY-004 (T5), C05-TEMPLES-IND-005 (T5) · X: C05-ARTPAR-001 (T5), C05-KRAM-1-033 (T5)
  2. **Hall and porches.** *Point:* the public interior, the balconied porches with seats and guardians, and the main east entrance.

## 4. Conclusion

- **Demonstrated:** the building as the fullest Bhūmija statement; its programme, by position; the readings, attributed.
- **Unresolved:**
  - the origin of the Bhūmija;
  - the shrine count;
  - the identifications of broken images;
  - measured dimensions: none exist in the corpus (Research Register RR-8).
- **Carried forward:** the temple names itself in writing; Ch 6 reads the writing.

## Evidence checklist

- **Plan:** C05-PANDE-001 to -010.
- **Tower:** C05-PANDE-014 to -020, C05-HARDY-005/-012.
- **Comparisons:** C05-TEMPLES-IND-017/-018, C05-ARTPAR-009 to -012.
- **Surfaces:** C05-PANDE-025 to -035.
- **Programme:** C05-PANDE-037 to -104.
- **Readings:** C05-PANDE-109 to -121, C05-HARDY-030/-032.
- **Flag-bearer:** C05-PANDE-036.

- RR-7: the Amareśvara's "1063".
- RR-8: a measured plan (Beglar, Cunningham after ingest).
- RR-9: the INTACH "82 × 42 m" figure, never used.

- **To Ch 7:** the hill's sculpture and the colossus.

# Chapter 6. Inscriptions and Intellectual Life: Epigraphy, Languages and Literary Culture

**Target:** 4,050 words · **Sections:** 8 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch06.jsonl` (223 direct, 177 contextual) · **Rules:** R1–R9, U1.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** What do Udaypur's inscriptions say, in what language and form, and who made them say it? What can these words establish about authority, patronage and learning, and what can they not?
- **Argument:** The stones are several archives with different makers and registers:
  - signed working records;
  - the king's poem of descent;
  - a grammar-serpent;
  - the grants of officials and governors, under Caulukya overlordship from 1163 to the early 1170s and Paramāra rule again by 1229 (U1);
  - pilgrims' notes;
  - Persian building records.

  Learning at Udaypur is evidenced as something displayed and used by rulers and their literate servants. It is not evidence of a school or a court in the town.
- **What changes:** from the inscription as a source of facts (Chs 1, 4) to the inscription as an act, made by named people for named ends.
- **Builds on:** §1.4 (the praśasti as object, the 1080 readings, the pilgrims, the first readers); Ch 4 (the praśasti's claims as event-claims). Recalled in clauses only.
- **Establishes for Ch 7:** the texts that date buildings (Amērā V.S. 1151; the mosque behind the temple, A.H. 737–39; the 1488 mosque; the Shahi Masjid, A.H. 1041). Ch 7 dates and describes the buildings without re-arguing the words.

## 2. Arc

The temple's first dated words (6.1) → the other early writing near Udaypur, and the faulty long inscription (6.2) → the praśasti as a poem (6.3) → whose poem it is and what its editors trust (6.4) → the serpent of letters (6.5) → the porch archive under changing masters (6.6) → after 1232, and the new language of Persian (6.7) → what the stones cannot say (6.8, into Ch 7).

Chronology is suspended in 6.1–6.5 (one generation, c. 1070–1100, arranged by kind of text) and runs in 6.6–6.7.

**Opening:** the six lines of V.S. 1137 at the east door, with their named poet.

**Closing:** the inscribed mosques, dated but not described (Ch 7).

## 3. Sections

### 6.1 Six lines at the door *(the 1080 record · 550 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows three things:
  - the temple's first dated words are a short, mostly prose Sanskrit record;
  - it names its poet, Mahipāla, son of Śṛṅgavāsa;
  - it records a completed flag-hoisting.

  It says nothing of who paid, who built or who attended, and no contemporary record calls the temple Nīlakaṇṭheśvara.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **A signed record.** *Point:* the six lines; the wish that the king make the earth "one umbrella"; the poet named; the flag-hoisting complete. The three readings of the date are recalled from §1.4.
     - E: C06-CII-006 (T1), C06-JAGTA-HI-001 (T8), C06-JAGTA-HI-008 (T8), C06-JAGTA-HI-009 (T8), C06-JAGTA-HI-010 (T8), C06-JAGTA-EN-034 (T8) · C: C06-JAGTA-EN-004 (T8), C06-JAGTA-EN-007 (T8), C06-JAGTA-EN-009 (T8), C06-JAGTA-EN-010 (T8), C06-PARTEMPLES-003 (T5) · X: C06-CII-025 (T1), C06-CII-029 (T1), C06-GANGULY-027 (T5), C06-JAGTA-EN-008 (T8) · Note: C06-JAGTA-EN-006 (T8), C06-JAGTA-HI-003 (T8)
  2. **What the form says.** *Point:* the king appears in a conventional wish, the temple is named after him, and the poet signs. The village's name for the temple is not the stones' name.
     - E: C06-JAGTA-HI-028 (T8), C06-JAGTA-EN-030 (T8) · C: C04-CII-119 (T1), C06-JAGTA-EN-027 (T8), C06-JAGTA-HI-007 (T8), C05-JAGTA-HI-006 (T8) · X: C04-CII-108 (T1) · Note: C06-PARTEMPLES-003 (T5)
- **Transition:** Udaypur's record is a working text; the province held finer verse and plainer.

### 6.2 Other early writing *(Vidisha's court verse; Amērā; Record C · 500 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows three things:
  - an official at Vidisha commissioned court-standard verse from Chhittapa;
  - a Brāhmaṇa's tank at Amērā, about 3 km south, carries verse by a *paṇḍita* whose son cut it (V.S. 1151);
  - the long V.S. 1116 inscription on the temple (Record C, R1, R3) is faulty in language and execution.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **Court verse in the province.** *Point:* Chhittapa, *mahākavi-cakravartin*, for a *daṇḍanāyaka*. No date is given.

  2. **A Brāhmaṇa's tank, a paṇḍita's verse.** *Point:* Vikrama's tank; the writer "-pāla" and his son Saumatika; never linked with Mahipāla (R6).

  3. **Record C as a text.** *Point:* twenty-two lines, faulty, and unedited in the corpus. Its quality is a reason for caution before it carries a date. Never merged with Records A or B (R1, R3).

- **Transition:** these short texts name their makers; the king's long poem has lost its maker.

### 6.3 A poem of descent *(the praśasti as composition · 600 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows three things:
  - the praśasti is metrical Sanskrit after one obeisance;
  - it opens with benedictions to Śiva in images;
  - it makes learning a royal virtue, from Vākpati's eloquence to Bhoja the *kavirāja* and Udayāditya as "another Sun".
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **Form.** *Point:* the text's form from CII; the two slabs recalled in a clause (§1.4).

  2. **Benediction and image.** *Point:* how the poem speaks.

## 4. Conclusion

- **Demonstrated:** several archives with different makers; learning displayed by rulers and their servants; the porch registering Caulukya and then Paramāra masters.
- **Unresolved:**
  - the praśasti's date and composer;
  - Record C's relation to Records A and B (R1, R3);
  - the lost and unpublished texts.
- **Carried forward:** dated buildings without descriptions.

# Chapter 7. The Wider Built Heritage of Udaypur: Fortifications, Shrines, Mosques and Water Structures

**Target:** 4,250 words · **Sections:** 7 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch07.jsonl` (239 direct, 107 contextual) · **Rules:** R5–R8.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** What do Udaypur's buildings other than the great temple record about who held the town, when, and for what? How much of that can be dated?
- **Argument:** The built fabric is a sequence of layers whose order is clearer than its dates. Only the mosques (1336–38, 1488, 1632) and one tank carry dates of their own. Survey periods are proposals (R7), and the survey's blanket "11th century" hides differences of build.
- **What changes:** from §1.1's walled town and remembered water tally, to a reader who knows which buildings are dated by inscription, which by fabric, and which only by proposal or memory.
- **Builds on:**
  - §1.1 (gates, houses, the tally);
  - §1.3 (the mosques' dates, the mansion);
  - §1.5 (the Bhujariya Talab, reused columns, the uncemented wall);
  - §2.4 (water at landscape level);
  - §3.3 P5 (the cave on the hill, a proposal) and §3.5 P4 (the hill's two-line inscription, a proposal);
  - §1.4 P3 (the flour-grinder's temple, as tradition).

  Recalled in clauses only.
- **Establishes for Ch 8:** the water built and used for drinking, fields and rites, and the tank as a work of merit.

## 2. Arc

The undated wall on the hill (7.1) → what was carved on the hill (7.2) → the walled town below (7.3) → shrines after the great temple (7.4) → the three dated mosques (7.5) → water on the hill and in the town (7.6) → the tanks, ending on the one tank with a contemporary record (7.7, into Ch 8).

The order runs from the least to the most closely dated. Chronology is suspended in 7.1–7.3 and runs inside 7.4–7.7.

**Opening:** a colonial observer's nameless wall (C07-GEOHER-002). The observer is to be named only after the ingest (R9).

**Closing:** Vikrama's tank and its stated uses.

## 3. Sections

### 7.1 A wall without a name *(the fortified hill · 750 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things:
  - the wall's fabric: dry sandstone, about 1,400 m, bastions, two gates, about 56 acres (the area conflict goes in a note);
  - what lay inside it, a lived and watered hill;
  - that its date is open: before Udayāditya or his, the survey's "tenth century" being a proposal;
  - that a lichen study has been proposed but not made.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **The unnamed wall.** *Point:* the object and its silence.

- **Transition (out):** water given as merit, into Ch 8's gift of land.

## 4. Conclusion

- **Demonstrated:** a sequence of layers, with dated mosques and one recorded tank.
- **Unresolved:** the wall's date; the colossus; the Moti Masjid; the Udayasamudra; most stepwell dates.
- **Carried forward:** what was done in and around these buildings (Ch 8).

# Chapter 8. Religion and the Material Life of Udaypur: Ritual, Agriculture, Trade and Craft

**Target:** 3,800 words · **Sections:** 7 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch08.jsonl` (44 direct, 225 contextual) · **Rules:** R4–R9, U1.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** What kept Udaypur's temple and town going, in land, water, craft, trade and service? What can be said of it for Udaypur itself, as distinct from the kingdom?
- **Argument:** Gifts, not a budget. Three dated endowments are recorded at Udaypur:
  - 1163, under the Caulukya overlordship (U1);
  - the early 1170s, Lūṇapasāka under Ajayapāla (U1);
  - 1229, under the Paramāra Devapāla.

  No founding endowment is known. Everything wider (tolls, shops, levies, workshops, trade) is the regional or normative pattern, labelled as such, and each section says once what it cannot show for Udaypur.
- **Builds on:** §1.1 (the time-keepers, the fair, living worship); Ch 6 (the porch texts); Ch 7 (Vikrama's tank and its uses).
- **Establishes for Ch 9:** service and memory held by a family; the people around the temple.

## 2. Arc

The gift of land in 1229 and the earlier gifts (8.1) → the regional pattern of endowment (8.2) → land, measure and water in the grants (8.3) → stone, cloth and the market in the Paramāra centuries (8.4) → the later trades (8.5, a bridge across centuries) → what worship required, and which Śaivism (8.6) → service at Udaypur, then and now (8.7, into Ch 9).

**Opening:** Dhāmadeva's four *biswa*, "taking water in hand", in 1229 (Part I, I.5).

**Closing:** the time-keepers' family and its hereditary service.

## 3. Sections

### 8.1 Four *biswa* at the eastern gate *(the Udaypur endowments under changing masters, 1163–1232 · 800 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things:
  - the gifts recorded at Udaypur: 1163 (Vasantapāla, when Kumārapāla's governor Rājyapāla held the eastern division, U1), the early 1170s (Lūṇapasāka under Ajayapāla, U1) and 1229 (Dhāmadeva, treasury officer of the Paramāra Devapāla);
  - that two of them drew on the Bhṛṅgārikā villages, attested from the 1170s (R4);
  - that Udaleśvara, Vaidyanātha and Udayeśvara are kept apart (R6);
  - that no founding endowment is known.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **The pillar record.** *Point:* the 1229 gift as an act; its date is from Ch 6 (§6.6).
     - E: C08-CII-020 (T1), C08-JAGTA-EN-008 (T8) · C: C06-CII-037 (T1) (home 6.6: the date), C08-JAGTA-EN-009 (T8), C08-JAGTA-EN-010 (T8), C08-JAGTA-HI-015 (T8) · Note: C08-JAGTA-HI-013 (T8) (Hindi original of the date), C06-CII-036 (T1) (alternative calendar reckoning to October 1230)
  2. **How small, and where.** *Point:* the *biswa* is glossed here, hedged once; Bhṛṅgāri is probably Bhiraṅgāvalī, about 6 km north-east (CII's identification).
     - E: C08-CII-039 (T1) · C: C06-CII-038 (T1) (home 6.6: the Bhṛṅgāri identification), C08-JAGTA-EN-011 (T8), C08-JAGTA-HI-014 (T8) · X: C08-JAGTA-EN-012 (T8) · Note: C08-JAGTA-EN-012 (T8) (Verma's gloss of *panchakula*, via Tiwari)
  3. **The earlier gifts, under the Caulukyas.** *Point:* 1163 and the early 1170s, framed as Caulukya overlordship (U1). The same group of villages gave sixty years apart.
     - E: C08-GANGULY-016 (T5), C08-GANGULY-015 (T5) · C: C06-CII-038 (T1), C01-CII-010 (T1) · Note: C08-GANGULY-016 (T5) (Ganguly's report only; not yet found in the CII items in the map), the Lūṇapasāka date range (1172 Ganguly / 1173 CII: "early 1170s", ROADMAP 18)
  4. **What is missing.** *Point:* the founder's endowment is unrecorded, and Ganguly's general report draws on praśasti language (R7).
     - E: C08-GANGULY-013 (T5), C08-JAGTA-HI-012 (T8) · C: C08-JAGTA-EN-007 (T8), C08-JAGTA-EN-013 (T8) · Note: C08-JAGTA-HI-017 (T8) (Verma comparison; Tiwari's "two decades" construction claim must NOT be carried: see flags), C08-JAGTA-EN-014 (T8) / C08-JAGTA-HI-016 (T8) (Tiwari's conjectures about the plot)
- **Transition:** how other Paramāra temples were kept shows what Udaypur's few records leave out.

### 8.2 What kept a Paramāra temple *(the regional pattern, labelled · 650 w)*

  - the nearest cases (a toll on bull-loads at Vidisha; bazaar rent at Bhilsa, reported);
  - the range of gifts across the kingdom;

# Chapter 9. Society and Cultural Life: Communities, Languages, Festivals and Oral Traditions

**Target:** 2,800 words (U2, approved exception) · **Sections:** 6 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch09.jsonl` (32 direct, 43 contextual) · **Rules:** R5–R8.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** Who lived at Udaypur, how did they speak and keep festival, and how does the town narrate itself?
- **Argument:** The evidence for communities and language is district-level or one writer's testimony, and the chapter says so. Its centre is memory:
  - who tells which stories;
  - how versions grow;
  - why Udaypur's own tales name only Udayāditya while the region's folklore belongs to Bhoja.
- **Builds on:** §1.1 (the fair, Berni Mata's plot, the snake legend, the 1870s shared shrine); §1.4–§1.5 (legends); Ch 8 (the time-keepers). Recalled in clauses only.
- **Establishes for Ch 10:** the town's own memory of decline, which Ch 10 tests against the record.

## 2. Arc

A lane of neighbours, now (9.1) → the society the town was founded into (9.2) → speech and the written word (9.3) → the festival year (9.4) → the tellers and the founder in the stories (9.5) → Bhoja's fame and the Garhi's threads, ending on the remembered deserted market of Daulatpur (9.6, into Ch 10).

**Opening:** the lane on the left as one leaves the temple: Hindu and Muslim households, described by community, never by residents' names.

**Closing:** Daulatpur, as memory only, with no location given.

  - the district's census figures, which are the only numbers.

  No counts from Tiwari.

# Chapter 10. Political Change and Economic Decline: Udaypur under Successive Regimes

**Target:** 4,200 words · **Sections:** 6 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch10.jsonl` (61 direct, 115 contextual), with the later-Paramāra items homed here by `chapters.md` · **Rules:** R2, R4–R9, U1, U7.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** When, and how, did Udaypur stop being a seat of power? What can "decline" mean for it?
- **Argument:** Udaypur was not destroyed by one conquest. It was lowered in rank, step by step over six centuries, while each new power still invested in it:
  - an administrative seat under several masters, including Caulukya governors 1163–early 1170s (U1);
  - a royal residence in the 1230s;
  - a *qasba* of Sarkar Chanderi;
  - a large village.

  Its economic decline, as distinct from its political demotion, is an inference the evidence cannot yet date.
- **Builds on:** §1.3, the sequence in outline, as corrected by U1. Every recalled event gets one clause; each paragraph's Point is new.
- **Establishes for Ch 11:** the Gazetteer's "once a place of considerable importance" as an official's description.

## 2. Arc

A seat that changed hands (10.1) → a king's residence on a shrinking frontier (10.2) → an end that took decades (10.3) → a *qasba* of Sarkar Chanderi (10.4) → revenue, brass and the railway (10.5) → what "decline" means (10.6, into Ch 11).

Chronology is the spine. Each period is asked the same four questions: rank, patronage, settlement and institutions. Chronology is suspended only in 10.6.

**Opening:** the V.S. 1220 record naming a Gujarat king at Udaypur (Part I, I.5).

**Closing:** the Gazetteer's sentence.

## 3. Sections

### 10.1 A seat that changed hands *(c. 1093–c. 1190 · 800 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things:
  - Naravarman's kingdom frayed on the Betwa;
  - the *mahākumāra* branch held the east "at least up to 1157";
  - Udaypur then passed to the Caulukya kings of Gujarat, who governed through it: Kumārapāla's *mahāsādhanika* Rājyapāla in 1163 (the title's first gloss in the book unless RR-42 moves it to §1.2, where it is used without one), and Lūṇapasāka under Ajayapāla in the early 1170s (U1);
  - Paramāra rule returned under Vindhyavarman, on a date not recorded for Udaypur.

  The Caulukya conquest of Malwa is dated 1138–45.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **A Gujarat king named at Udaypur.** *Point:* the V.S. 1220 record and the chapter's question.
     - E: C10-PARTEMPLES-001 (T5), C10-CII-002 (T1) · C: C08-GANGULY-016 (T5) · Note: C06-GANGULY-034 (T5) (Ganguly's Kumārapāla record of 1163; possibly the same stone; not merged), C01-GANGULY-015 (T5) (Ganguly's "eastern division" framing, attributed)
  2. **A kingdom fraying at its eastern edge.** *Point:* Naravarman and the Candellas on the Betwa.
     - E: C04-CII-133 (T1), C04-CII-136 (T1), C04-CII-135 (T1) · C: C04-GANGULY-125 (T5), C04-PARTEMPLES-012 (T5), C04-GANGULY-122 (T5) · X: C04-CII-142 (T1), C04-CII-148 (T1), C01-GANGULY-005 (T5) · Note: C04-GANGULY-123 (T5) (Ganguly's "degeneration of its military strength", attributed)
  3. **The branch that held the east.** *Point:* the *mahākumāra*s, to 1157.
     - E: C04-CII-146 (T1), C04-CII-149 (T1), C04-CII-150 (T1), C10-CII-002 (T1) · C: C04-CII-151 (T1), C06-CII-046 (T1), C04-PARTEMPLES-015 (T5), C01-KB-CII-008 (T1) · X: C04-CII-141 (T1), C04-GANGULY-129 (T5), C10-CII-001 (T1) · Note: C04-CII-141 (T1) vs C04-CII-176 (T1), C10-GANGULY-008 (T5) (1138 vs 1145 for the conquest; a range "1138–45")
  4. **A Caulukya headquarters.** *Point:* the Caulukyas governed through the seat (U1). The 1163 record and the V.S. 1220 record are cited together, never merged (R6); Udaleśvara is not identified (R6).
     - E: C10-PARTEMPLES-001 (T5), C10-CII-002 (T1) · C: C08-GANGULY-015 (T5) (home Ch 8: cross-reference), C08-GANGULY-016 (T5) (home Ch 8: cross-reference), C01-GANGULY-010 (T5), C06-CII-038 (T1), C01-GANGULY-015 (T5) · X: C04-GANGULY-132 (T5), C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-024 (T9), C04-ARTPAR-005 (T5), C04-PARTEMPLES-013 (T5) · Note: Lūṇapasāka's year, 1171/1172/1173 (C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-003 (T9), C01-GANGULY-010 (T5), C01-CII-010 (T1)), and the Gazetteer's attribution of a 1173 Bhṛṅgārikā record to Udayāditya (C01-GAZ-010 (T7)), which the book does not follow.
  5. **Recovered, and reunited.** *Point:* Vindhyavarman and Devapāla's succession.
     - E: C04-GANGULY-135 (T5), C04-CII-161 (T1), C04-GANGULY-134 (T5) · C: C04-CII-162 (T1), C04-CII-163 (T1) · X: C04-GANGULY-131 (T5), C04-GANGULY-133 (T5)
- **Transition:** the reunited house appears at Udaypur with the king in residence.

### 10.2 A king's residence on a shrinking frontier *(c. 1190–1305 · 800 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows three things:
  - Devapāla, a prince's line become king, resided at Udayapura in the early 1230s;
  - his records gave land around it (their texts are §6.6's; the gifts §8.1's);
  - Bhilsa was lost and retaken, the kingdom pressed from three sides, and Bhilsa fell again in the early 1290s.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **From prince's line to king.** *Point:* Devapāla.

  3. **Bhilsa lost and retaken.** *Point:* Iltutmish in 1233–34.

  4. **Pressed from three sides.** *Point:* Delhi, the Yādavas and Gujarat.
     - E: C10-CII-004 (T1), C04-CII-166 (T1) · C: C10-GANGULY-004 (T5), C01-KB-CII-014 (T1), C06-GANGULY-030 (T5) · X: C04-GANGULY-138 (T5), C10-PARMAR-RAJPUT-001 (T9) (omit in text; tier 9)
  5. **Bhilsa falls again.** *Point:* the early 1290s raid; 1305.
     - E: C10-GAZ-003 (T7), C10-MADHYABHARAT-002 (T5) · C: C10-GANGULY-005 (T5), C04-GANGULY-141 (T5) · X: C10-GAZ-013 (T7) · Note: the Bhilsa raid date, 1290 (C10-GAZ-013 (T7)), 1291 (C10-JAGTA-EN-003 (T8)), 1293 (C10-GAZ-003 (T7)): "early 1290s"; C10-TEMPLE-ECON-002 (T5)'s "1292" is excluded (Temple Economics is barred).
- **Transition:** after 1305 a Paramāra title still appears on Udaypur's stones.

### 10.3 An end that took decades *(1305–c. 1530 · 850 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things:
  - a Mahārājādhirāja Jayasiṃha was recorded inside the temple's east entrance c. 1309–10 (Ganguly's report);
  - the Tughluq mosque of 1336–38 inserted a new institution but left the shrine in worship;
  - under the Malwa Sultanate the town was fought over but not named, while Sanskrit record-making went on;
  - the survival legends set against this record, in one paragraph (the conflict's home for testing them).

## 4. Conclusion

- **Unresolved:**
  - the date of the Paramāra return;
  - who held Udaypur 1713–1948;
  - the farman's original;
  - V.S. 1841.
- **Carried forward:** the town as described by officials.

# Chapter 11. Udaypur in the Modern Period: Archaeology, Heritage Administration and Conservation

**Target:** 4,250 words · **Sections:** 6 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch11.jsonl` (92 direct, 17 contextual), with the conservation history moved here from the Afterword's map · **Rules:** R5–R9, U7.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** How was Udaypur made known and protected, from the first survey report to the documentation of 2021?
- **Argument:** Made known and protected in pieces. Each intervention covered one object, never the town:
  - a report;
  - a museum removal;
  - a list entry;
  - one clearance;
  - a survey.

  The record of loss to 2021 is told by kind of threat. Remedies are the Afterword's.
- **Builds on:** §1.3 (the brass cover, "Garde Sahib", custody today) and §1.4 (Beglar, Cunningham, Bühler; Garde's find; the reset slab). Each is recalled in a clause and adds only its effects.
- **Establishes for Ch 12:** the people the record meets at its edges.

## 2. Arc

Into the reports, 1871–1888 (11.1) → Gwalior State's department (11.2) → protected in parts after 1947 (11.3) → counting the town, 2020–21 (11.4) → the palace and the order, 2021 (11.5) → the record of loss by threat (11.6, into Ch 12).

**Opening:** Beglar's tour of 1871–72.

**Closing:** the households and occupants at the record's edges. No recommendation.

## 3. Sections

### 11.1 Into the reports *(the antiquarian tours, 1871–1888 · 800 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows three things:
  - what Beglar set down, and his Aurangzeb attribution corrected by Cunningham;
  - the readings that became fixtures;
  - how the reports made the temple a standard reference.

  The reports are quoted only through Pande until the ingest (R9).

  3. **Readings that became fixtures.** *Point:* Cunningham's functions for the precinct.
     - C: C05-GANGULY-012 (T5), C05-PANDE-008 (T5) (both homed in Ch 5; this paragraph recalls them as transmission, with no establishing item of its own) · Note: C05-PANDE-008 (T5) (the reading is Cunningham's; Ch 5 decides the hall's function)
  4. **From report to model.** *Point:* Kramrisch and the handbooks.

# Chapter 12. Udaypur Today: A Living Community and Its Historic Environment

**Target:** 3,750 words · **Sections:** 6 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch12.jsonl` (71 direct, 6 contextual), with the wildlife items moved here from Ch 2 · **Rules:** R5–R8, U7.

## 1. Chapter function

- **Central question:** How do the people of Udaypur live with a thousand-year-old town that others visit as a monument, and whose account of that life does the evidence give?
- **Argument:**
  - Udaypur in the early 2020s was a working village living inside and on top of its medieval town and hill.
  - Visitors and residents rarely met.
  - The record speaks about residents more than for them.
  - The 2021 lane widening is the dated episode where residents' own acts show.

  "Today" means the last record, c. early 2021, stated once.
- **Builds on:** §1.1 (census, houses, the shops, worship, the fair) and §2.3/§2.5 (vegetation, the quarries, Govind Saxena). Recalled in clauses only.
- **Establishes for the Afterword:** residents' hopes and outsiders' plans.

## 2. Arc

A home made from a ruin (12.1) → fields, market and quarries (12.2) → the living hill (12.3) → the temple's visitors (12.4) → the town becoming visible, 2020–21 (12.5) → the lane to the temple, 2021, and whose voice (12.6, into the Afterword).

The scale moves from the household to the village, the hill, Bhopal and back to one lane.

**Opening:** a family's house, found buried in an excavation decades ago, cleaned out and lived in.

**Closing:** residents expecting visitors and trade; outsiders drawing up plans. No recommendation.

## 3. Sections

### 12.1 Living inside the old town *(the settlement, 1901 to c. 2021 · 750 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things:
  - a household's home made from a ruin;
  - the documented growth: under two thousand in 1901 (928 or 1,619, Research Register RR-26), 2,025 in 1971, 6,383 in 2011, with no testimony counts (R5);
  - a village with an office and a school;
  - patches of old and new.
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **A home made from a ruin.** *Point:* the opening household.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-014 (T8) · X: C01-KB-INTACH-001 (T7) (recall only: 1.1's twelve-courtyard house, one clause at most) · C: C07-JAGTA-EN-037 (T8)
  2. **Seventy years of growth.** *Point:* the documented counts only (conflict home: population).
     - E: C12-GAZ-008 (T7), C12-GEOHER-001 (T7) · C: C10-GAZ-052 (T7), C10-GAZ-053 (T7), C12-INTACH-002 (T7) (area 1,966 ha; 6,383 via villageinfo.in) · X: C12-GAZ-002 (T7), C12-GAZ-004 (T7) · Note: C12-GAZ-008 (T7) (928 vs 1,619 printed for 1901)
  3. **A village with an office and a school.** *Point:* the place as the state sees it.
     - E: C12-GAZ-005 (T7), C12-GAZ-006 (T7), C12-JAGTA-HI-005 (T8) · C: C12-INTACH-001 (T7) · X: C12-JAGTA-HI-004 (T8) (constituency only; **no distances from it**)
  4. **Patches of old and new.** *Point:* history in the foundations.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-007 (T8) · C: C12-JAGTA-HI-006 (T8) (survival of old buildings "to a considerable extent"; **drop the "eight thousand"**) · X: C07-JAGTA-HI-051 (T8) (recall)
- **Transition:** the village works its fields and its stone.

### 12.2 Fields, a market town and the quarries *(livelihoods at the last record · 650 w)*

  - fields pressing on the forest.
