# Afterword. Conserving Udaypur: Community, Heritage and the Future

**Target:** 2,750 words (U3) · **Sections:** 6 · **Evidence:** `evidence_index/ch13.jsonl` (58 direct, 9 contextual), with recalls from Chs 11–12 · **Rules:** R5–R9, U7.

## 1. Function

- **Question:** What does the record add up to for the town's future: conservation of what, for whom, and at whose cost?
- **Argument:** The Afterword is built, not summarised.
  - It fixes the evidence horizon (early 2021, and a survey made after 2021).
  - It maps jurisdiction and risk in compressed form, recalling Ch 11 without re-describing it.
  - It sorts the proposals on record and tests them against the book's own evidence.
  - It assigns roles by what parties have done.
  - Only its last section argues, labelled once: Udaypur survived by being used, so conserve it as a lived-in town and hill.
- **Distinguishes:**
  - what concludes the historical narrative (the horizon);
  - what emerges from the book as a whole (the protection gap; the voice problem);
  - callbacks by content to §1.1, Ch 7, Ch 10 and Ch 11;
  - what would be redundant (Ch 11's findings; Ch 12's lane).

## 2. Arc

Where the record stops (AW.1) → what is at stake, and who answers for it (AW.2) → the proposals on record (AW.3) → testing them against the book (AW.4) → who keeps Udaypur (AW.5) → what the history argues (AW.6, ending on the village under the temple).

**Opening:** Tiwari's 2019 letter to the Collector.

**Closing:** the village under the temple, by content. No exhortation, no question, no claim beyond the last record.

## 3. Sections

### AW.1 Where the record stops *(2019 to the survey · 400 w)*
- **Statement:** By the end, the reader knows two things:
  - the dated sequence of the last record (2019 letter; 2021 events, one clause each);
  - that nothing in the evidence is later than the survey, whose own date must be found first (Research Register RR-31).
- **Paragraphs:**
  1. **A letter to a Collector.** *Point:* the earliest dated proposal.
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-001 (T8) · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-002 (T8) · X: C13-JAGTA-HI-004 (T8)
  2. **2021 in one sentence each.** *Point:* recall without retelling.
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-008 (T8), C13-JAGTA-HI-021 (T8) · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-007 (T8), C13-JAGTA-EN-014 (T8) · X: C11-JAGTA-EN-027 (T8) (recall: the order's date, 15 Jan 2021), C12-JAGTA-EN-008 (T8) (recall), C11-GEOHER-006 (T7)
  3. **A historian's death and a plan under consideration.** *Point:* Suresh Mishra (never P. K. Mishra, R6); the guest voices are checked first (RR-30).
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-013 (T8), C13-JAGTA-HI-018 (T8) · X: C13-JAGTA-HI-038 (T8) (the tribute's phrase "a historian's last dream"; quote at most once in the book, preferably in AW.5)
  4. **The horizon.** *Point:* stated once.

# Part III. Cross-Chapter Architecture

## A. The chain of chapters (last move → first move)

For Chapters 1–4 the lines are the text's own. For later chapters they are the entries' planned opening and closing. "Agrees" means the two sides agree on argument and on place.

| Join | Last move | First move | Argument / place | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 → 2 | "Whoever first lived here, under his rule or before it, had to make terms with that stone and that water." | the Betwa as the Vētravatī of the inscriptions; what the land gave and withheld | stone and water → water first, then stone; Udaypur → its river | agrees |
| 2 → 3 | "…whether anyone had used it before Udaypur had its name, the ground itself does not say." | "The oldest human traces in the country around Udaypur lie in its river valleys." | the ground's silence → the oldest traces, and how little reaches the hill | agrees |
| 3 → 4 | "Who held the hill itself in those years is not known." | "The next claim made about this country comes from the west": Sīyaka II's eastern limit at Vidisha | the open question → the house that took the frontier, the gap kept open | agrees |
| 4 → 5 | the temple that bears his name, the one work a record of the time ties to the reign | the temple facing east, entered today from the west | the dated patronage → the building as built | agrees |
| 5 → 6 | the flag-bearer and the inscribed name at the east doorway, not read | the six lines of V.S. 1137 and their named poet | the unread name → its reading | agrees |
| 6 → 7 | what the stones cannot say; the inscribed mosques, dated but undescribed | the nameless wall on the hill, as a colonial observer saw it (RR-15) | the inscribed → the uninscribed | agrees |
| 7 → 8 | Vikrama's tank and its stated uses | Dhāmadeva's four *biswa*, "taking water in hand", 1229 | water given as merit → land given to a god | agrees |
| 8 → 9 | the time-keepers' family and its hereditary service | a lane of Hindu and Muslim neighbours, by community | a family's service → the community around it | agrees |
| 9 → 10 | the remembered deserted market of Daulatpur, memory only | the V.S. 1220 record naming a Gujarat king at Udaypur | memory → the record | agrees |
| 10 → 11 | the Gazetteer's "once a place of considerable importance" (the phrase §1.1 quotes) | Beglar's tour, 1871–72 | an official's verdict → the history of description | agrees (an echo of §1.1, recalled and not reintroduced) |
| 11 → 12 | the households and occupants at the record's edges | a family's house, found in an excavation, cleaned out and lived in | the people at the edges → one household | agrees |
| 12 → AW | residents expecting visitors; outsiders drawing up plans | Tiwari's 2019 letter to the Collector | plans → the earliest dated plan | agrees |

**Rules for every join:** no recap, no announcement, no rhetorical question, no closing aphorism.

**Light-touch items found at the joins:** none. The text-side issues found elsewhere are listed in RR-42.

## B. The principal argument, Chapter 1 to the Afterword

1. **Ch 1.** An ordinary village lives inside a king's town, and everything it keeps reaches back to one eleventh-century act of naming, which may have been a founding or a taking-over.
2. **Ch 2.** The land made that town possible without deciding it: water had to be made, and the town was cut from its own hill.
3. **Ch 3.** The country was old, named and carved long before, but its past reaches the hill only as proposals, and by the tenth century the range was a crowded frontier.
4. **Ch 4.** A king's name and a king's temple are certain; a king's town is not.
5. **Ch 5.** The building is read for its own making.
6. **Ch 6.** The writing is read for who made it speak.
7. **Ch 7.** The rest of the fabric is an order of layers more than a calendar.
8. **Ch 8.** What kept the temple was gifts, not a budget.
9. **Ch 9.** The town narrates itself.
10. **Ch 10.** It lost rank in steps under successive masters, the Caulukya kings among them in the twelfth century (U1).
11. **Ch 11.** It was made known and protected in pieces.
12. **Ch 12.** It lives on as a working village in an old town, seen mostly by others.
13. **Afterword.** The book argues, once and labelled, for conservation by use.

## C. Threads, and where each advances

1. **Founded or taken over:** §1.5 (the question) → §2.5 (the ground does not say) → §3.3 P5, §3.5 P4 (the hill's proposals) → §4.5 P1–P2 (the name; Bhringrajpur) → §7.1 (the wall) → §7.7 (the Udayasamudra) → §9.5 (the founder in the stories) → AW.5 (as research). Never closed.
2. **Claim and record (R7):** §1.4 (the praśasti's claims) → §4.1–§4.3 → §6.3–§6.4 → §9.5–§9.6 (tradition) → Chs 7 and 11 (survey proposals).
3. **The triad of names:** §1.2 P3 → §2.4 P3 (the tank) → §4.5 P1 (the town) → §6.1 (the stones never say Nīlakaṇṭheśvara; the later names in §6.6) → §7.7 (the Udayasamudra unidentified).
4. **Rank, not ruin:** §1.1 (the 1979 verdict) → §1.2 P4 and §1.3 (the seat and its masters) → §4.5 P3–P4 → §6.6 → §7.5 → Ch 10 (home) → §12.1.
5. **Continuity of worship:** §1.1 P5 → §3.3 (the Śaiva images before the Paramāras) → §5.1 → §8.6–§8.7 → §10.3 → §12.4.
6. **Water:** §1.5 P4 → §2.1–§2.4 → §7.6–§7.7 → §8.3 → §12.3 → AW.3.
7. **Stone:** §2.3, §2.5 → §5.4 (craft) → §7 (fabric) → §12.2 (quarries, U7).
8. **Memory and its tellers (R5):** §1.1 → §1.4 P3 → §9.5–§9.6 (home) → Ch 12 → AW.5.
9. **Limits of the record:** §3.1 P1 (no find on the hill) → §3.5 P5 → §6.8 → AW.1 (the horizon).
10. **Protected in pieces:** §1.3 P4 (custody) → Ch 11 → AW.2–AW.4.
11. **Whose voice:** §9.1 → §12.6 → AW.5.

## G. Chronological overlaps, signposted

- **Before c. 900:** told in Ch 3. §2 uses the land only, and §1.5 the proposals for an older place.
- **c. 900–1093:** told in Ch 4. §1.2 gives the outline; §5–§6 use the reign's dated record.
- **1093–1305:** told in Ch 10. §1.3 gives the outline; Chs 6 and 8 use the same records for their own question.
- **1336–1632:** told in §1.3 (outline) and Ch 10. §6.7, §7.5 and §10.3–§10.4 each add only their own layer.
- **1871–2021:** Ch 11 (acts), Ch 12 (residents), AW (consequences). §1.4 names the first readers only.

## H. Echoes planted and paid off (one each)

- The 1979 "once a place of considerable importance": planted in §1.1 P1, paid off at the close of Ch 10. Ch 10 recalls it as the phrase the book began with.
- "Who held the hill itself in those years is not known" (§3.5): answered only as far as §4.1 allows, a kingdom's edge and not the hill's holder.
- The temple "facing east" (§1.1 P5): Ch 5 opens on the entry from the west, and Ch 6 at the east door.
- Stone and water (§1.5 P4): Ch 2's structure, and the Afterword's case for conservation by use.

## I. Redundancies removed (checked against the earlier outlines)

1. **The governors of the 1160s–70s.** v1 named Rājyapāla in four places, each framed its own way. U1 gives one wording. §1.2–§1.3 state it, §10.1 tells it, and §6.6 and §8.1 recall it.
2. **The hill's sculpture.** It is its own section, §7.2, instead of a paragraph added at merge.
3. **The proposals and the book's test of them.** They are separated: the proposals are attributed (AW.3), and the assessment is labelled (AW.4).
4. **The kinship.** It is settled once (U11). Ch 4 states it from the inscriptions and does not repeat §1.2's note.

# Part IV. Downstream Drafting Protocol

*(Applies when the pause is lifted.)*

1. **Authority:**
   - `chapters.md`;
   - the decisions U1–U13;
   - the rules R1–R11;
   - this outline;
   - `style/style_profile.md` and `style/style_sheet.md`.
2. **No paid API calls (U5).**
   - Use only the free checks: `inhouse.py check`, which includes the prose checks, the dynasty-spelling check and the ledger check.
   - `ledger.py update` is not run. Each chapter's ledger is written by hand and merged with `ledger.py merge`.
   - The Chroma vector collection is not queried, since every query costs an embedding call.
3. **Per chapter:**
   1. read the entry and Part I;
   2. print the quotations of every cited item (`_tools/quotes.py`);
   3. write `chNN/chNN_plan_v1.md`, with headings `## N.x Title *(scope · NNN w)*`;
   4. make the cfg (model `ch04/cfg_ch04v1.py`, BUDGET 0) and the pack (`_pipe/extend_pack.py`);
   5. draft `run/chN/N.x.md` section by section;
   6. verify in-session;
   7. run the checks;
   8. stitch (`_tools/stitch.py N`) and build (`_pipe/build_outputs.py --chapters N`).
4. **Structure.** Each chapter keeps the number and shape of sections its entry sets. A drafting session does not add, remove, merge or reorder sections; that is a structural change for the user.
   - A section may land at 70–120% of its target.
   - Never pad. If the evidence runs out, report the shortfall with its reason.
5. **Chapters already written (1–4).** Changes to them are light-touch fixes listed in RR-42, or decided revisions (U12, RR-40). After any change: run `_tools/reconcile.py`, which re-derives the as-written entry, then `_tools/delivered_entries.py` and the assembler.
6. **In-session verification.**
   - Every factual sentence is checked against its cited items' quotations.
   - Every quotation is matched verbatim (the validator checks this).
   - Pages come from the item locators.
   - Anything unverifiable is logged in `chNN_flags.md`.
   - Every deliverable says "verified in-session, not by an independent model".
7. **Rules cited, not restated.**
   - R1–R11 and U1–U13 govern every chapter.
   - Karṇa always takes his dynasty (R2), in IAST (U10).
   - Administrative terms are dated (R4).
   - Records A, B and C stay separate (R1, R3).
   - The Gazetteer's claims are its own (U8).
   - No testimony supplies a measure (R5).
   - The settled ranges are used as ranges (R10).
8. **Evidence roles.**
   - E items establish, and only in their home unit (R6).
   - C items corroborate or cross-refer; X items give context; Note items feed `{{note: …}}`.
   - Items marked optional are omitted unless a section would land below 70%.
9. **Gap-filling.** Free local text or BM25 search only, through `_tools/kb_new.py`: verbatim text, page from the marker, tier, `kb-new`. If local search cannot answer, add a research flag; never spend money.
10. **What needs the user:**
    - any structural change;
    - any conflict this outline keeps open;
    - the legal-review passages (LR-1, LR-2);
    - D5–D7.

# Part VI. Continuity and Duplication Checklist

**Per section:**
- [ ] The quotations of all cited items have been read, every quotation matched verbatim, and unverifiable items logged.
- [ ] The section's E items are E only here (R6); cross-references are a clause.
- [ ] R2 and U10 (Karṇa, by dynasty, in IAST), R4 (dated terms), R1/R3 (Records A–C), R5 (no testimony measures) and R10 (ranges) hold.
- [ ] The Gazetteer supplies at most 40% of cited items (R8), or the warning is recorded (accepted for §8.5 and §10.5).
- [ ] Recall of Chs 1–4 is in clauses (their Part II entries; Part I, I.4).
- [ ] Terms already glossed (Part III, E) are not re-glossed.
- [ ] The section lands at 70–120% of target, with no padding.

**Per chapter:**
- [ ] The first paragraph takes up the previous chapter's last (Part III, A).
- [ ] The last paragraph keeps its prohibitions.
- [ ] The ledger is merged by hand.
- [ ] The report states "verified in-session, not by an independent model".
- [ ] The legal-review passages are listed (Chs 10–12).

**Book-wide:**
- [ ] The east door opens Ch 6 only.
- [ ] U1 wording agrees in §1.2, §1.3, §3.4, §6.6, §8.1 and §10.1.
- [ ] U11 wording agrees in §1.2 and §4.3.
- [ ] No conflict is resolved silently (Part I, I.3).
- [ ] Retired material stays out (Part I, I.7), and excluded sources stay out (R9).
- [ ] The two Mishras are kept apart.
- [ ] The evidence horizon is stated once (AW.1).
